The following description:

- The Zkr extension **adds** the **SSEED** and **USEED** fields to the **mseccfg** CSR **to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes** (see Privileged ISA specification).

- **Implementations may implement** **mseccfg** such that **[s,u]seed is a read‑only constant value 0**.

- **Software may discover** if **access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode** **by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.  
RULE 2: Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to the [s,u]seed fields of mseccfg and reading back the result.

The following description:

- The direct **mseccfg.[s,u]seed** option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.  
- This option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves.  
- Modules that use the **mseccfg.[s,u]seed** option can enforce the entire key lifecycle—from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization)—to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option permits drawing a security boundary around a component concerning Sensitive Security Parameter flows, even when the component is not executing in M mode.  
RULE 2: The mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is intended to be useful for implementing trusted enclaves.  
RULE 3: Modules that employ the mseccfg.[s,u]seed option can enforce that the full key lifecycle, from generation in the entropy source to zeroization, occurs without the key being transferred across the security boundary to external code.

The following description:

- CSR 0x747 (MRW) is named **mseccfg**; its description is "Machine security configuration register."  
- CSR 0x757 (MRW) is named **mseccfgh**; its description is "Upper 32 bits of mseccfg, RV32 only."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The CSR located at address 0x747 is named mseccfg, is a machine‑mode read‑write register, and provides machine security configuration.  
RULE 2: The textual description of the CSR named mseccfg is "Machine security configuration register".  
RULE 3: The CSR located at address 0x757 is named mseccfgh, is a machine‑mode read‑write register, and supplies the upper 32 bits of mseccfg.  
RULE 4: The CSR named mseccfgh, which supplies the upper 32 bits of mseccfg, is defined only for RV32 implementations.
======================================================= END - 3 ========================================================

The following description:

- **Register existence**:  
  *"mseccfg is a 64‑bit read/write register, formatted as shown in Figure 26, that controls security features. It exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented. Otherwise, it is reserved."*

- **Zkr extension fields**:  
  *"The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M."*  

  *"When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception."*  
  *"When USEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from U‑mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception."*  
  *"If Zkr or U‑mode is not implemented, USEED is read‑only zero."*  

  *"When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception."*  
  *"When SSEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception."*  
  *"If Zkr or S‑mode is not implemented, SSEED is read‑only zero."*

- **Table 20 – Entropy Source Access Control (rules derived from the USEED and SSEED bits)**  

  | Condition (mseccfg bits) | Architectural effect |
  |--------------------------|----------------------|
  | M‑mode (no USEED/SSEED needed) | "The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal (with a CSR read‑write instruction.). Attempted read without a write raises an illegal‑instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits." |
  | U‑mode, USEED = 0 | "Any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception." |
  | U‑mode, USEED = 1 | "The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read‑write." |
  | S/HS‑mode, SSEED = 0 | "Any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception." |
  | S/HS‑mode, SSEED = 1 | "The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read‑write." |
  | VS/VU‑mode, SSEED = 0 | "Any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception." |
  | VS/VU‑mode, SSEED = 1 | "A read‑write seed access raises a virtual‑instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal‑instruction exception." |

- **Smepmp extension fields**:  

  *"The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and the MML fields in mseccfg."*  

  - **RLB (Rule Locking Bypass)**:  
    *"When mseccfg.RLB … is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited."*  
    *"When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), then mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset."*  

  - **MMWP (Machine‑Mode Allowlist Policy)**:  
    *"The mseccfg.MMWP … is a WARL field. … This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset."*  
    *"When set it changes the default PMP policy for M‑mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule, to denied instead of ignored."*  

  - **MML (Machine Mode Lockdown)**:  
    *"The mseccfg.MML … is a WARL field. … This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset."*  
    *"When mseccfg.MML is set the system's behavior changes in the following way:"*  

    1. *"The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes: Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, it now marks a rule as M‑mode‑only when set and S/U‑mode‑only when unset. The formerly reserved encoding of pmpcfg.RW=01, and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111, now encode a Shared‑Region."*  

       - *"An M‑mode‑only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode. It also remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set."*  

       - *"An S/U‑mode‑only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode."*  

       - *"A Shared‑Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits:"*  
         * "A Shared‑Region rule where pmpcfg.L is not set can be used for sharing data between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, so is not executable. M‑mode has read/write access to that region, and S/U‑mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set."  
         * "A Shared‑Region rule where pmpcfg.L is set can be used for sharing code between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, so is not writable. Both M‑mode and S/U‑mode have execute access on the region, and M‑mode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set. The rule remains locked … unless mseccfg.RLB is set."  
         * "The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, where both modes only have read‑only access to the region. The rule remains locked … unless mseccfg.RLB is set."  

    2. *"Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M‑mode‑only or a locked Shared‑Region is not possible and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged. This restriction can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB e.g. during the boot process."*  

    3. *"Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M‑mode‑only rule or a locked Shared‑Region rule with executable privileges. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U‑mode‑only rule is denied."*  

    4. *"If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination of pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use."*

- **Smmpm extension field**:  

  *"If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field enables or disables pointer masking (see Chapter 25) for M‑mode according to the values in Table 21. If Smmpm is not implemented, PMM is read‑only zero. The PMM field is read‑only zero for RV32."*  

  **Table 21 – Legal values of PMM WARL field**

  | Value | Description |
  |-------|-------------|
  | 00 | Pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0) |
  | 01 | Reserved |
  | 10 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN ‑ 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64) |
  | 11 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN ‑ 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64) |

- **Zicfilp extension field**:  

  *"The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg."*  
  *"When MLPE field is 1, Zicfilp extension is enabled in M‑mode."*  
  *"When the MLPE field is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M‑mode and the following rules apply to M‑mode:"*  
    - *"The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED."*  
    - *"The LPAD instruction operates as a no‑op."*

- **XLEN‑dependent alias register**:  

  *"When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is a 32‑bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg. Register mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN=64."*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64‑bit read/write register that controls security features and it exists only if any extension adds a field to mseccfg; otherwise it is reserved.  
RULE 2: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to mseccfg to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.  
RULE 3: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, any access to the seed CSR in U‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 4: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from U‑mode is allowed and all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 5: If the Zkr extension or U‑mode is not implemented, the USEED field in mseccfg is read‑only zero.  
RULE 6: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any access to the seed CSR from S‑mode or HS‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 7: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from S‑mode or HS‑mode is allowed and all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 8: If the Zkr extension or S‑mode is not implemented, the SSEED field in mseccfg is read‑only zero.  
RULE 9: In M‑mode, the seed CSR is always available as a normal read‑write CSR instruction; a read‑only access to the seed CSR raises an illegal‑instruction exception regardless of mseccfg bits.  
RULE 10: In U‑mode with mseccfg.USEED = 0, any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 11: In U‑mode with mseccfg.USEED = 1, the seed CSR is accessible as normal and no exception is raised for read‑write accesses.  
RULE 12: In S‑/HS‑mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 0, any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 13: In S‑/HS‑mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 1, the seed CSR is accessible as normal and no exception is raised for read‑write accesses.  
RULE 14: In VS‑/VU‑mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 0, any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 15: In VS‑/VU‑mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 1, a read‑write seed CSR access raises a virtual‑instruction exception while all other access types raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 16: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields to mseccfg.  
RULE 17: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.  
RULE 18: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and any pmpcfg.L bit is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and further writes to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.  
RULE 19: mseccfg.MMWP is a WARL field and a sticky bit, meaning that once mseccfg.MMWP is set it cannot be cleared until a PMP reset.  
RULE 20: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, the default PMP policy for Machine mode on memory accesses that lack a matching PMP rule changes from ignored to denied.  
RULE 21: mseccfg.MML is a WARL field and a sticky bit, meaning that once mseccfg.MML is set it cannot be cleared until a PMP reset.  
RULE 22: When mseccfg.MML is set, the meaning of pmpcfg.L changes so that a set pmpcfg.L marks a rule as M‑mode‑only and a cleared pmpcfg.L marks a rule as S/U‑mode‑only, and the previously reserved encoding pmpcfg.RW=01 and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 denote a Shared‑Region.  
RULE 23: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M‑mode‑only PMP rule is enforced in Machine mode, denied in Supervisor and User modes, and remains locked against further modifications until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.  
RULE 24: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U‑mode‑only PMP rule is enforced in Supervisor and User modes and denied in Machine mode.  
RULE 25: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared‑Region PMP rule with pmpcfg.L clear is non‑executable; Machine mode has read/write access, and Supervisor/User mode has read‑only access if pmpcfg.X is clear or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.  
RULE 26: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared‑Region PMP rule with pmpcfg.L set is used for code sharing, is non‑writable, provides execute access to both Machine and Supervisor/User modes, gives Machine mode additional read access if pmpcfg.X is set, and remains locked unless mseccfg.RLB is set.  
RULE 27: When mseccfg.MML is set, a PMP rule encoded as pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 denotes a Shared‑Region for data sharing where both Machine and Supervisor/User modes have read‑only access, and the rule remains locked unless mseccfg.RLB is set.  
RULE 28: When mseccfg.MML is set, attempts to write a PMP rule with executable privileges that would be M‑mode‑only or a locked Shared‑Region are ignored and leave pmpcfg unchanged, unless mseccfg.RLB is set which can temporarily lift the restriction.  
RULE 29: When mseccfg.MML is set, execution of Machine‑mode code is permitted only from regions governed by a matching M‑mode‑only PMP rule or a locked Shared‑Region rule with executable privileges; execution from regions without such a rule or governed by an S/U‑mode‑only rule is denied.  
RULE 30: When mseccfg.MML is not set, the encoding pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.  
RULE 31: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field in mseccfg selects pointer masking enablement for Machine mode as defined by its WARL encoding; otherwise the PMM field in mseccfg is read‑only zero, and it is also read‑only zero for RV32.  
RULE 32: When mseccfg.PMM = 00, pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0).  
RULE 33: When mseccfg.PMM = 10, pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN − 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64).  
RULE 34: When mseccfg.PMM = 11, pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN − 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64).  
RULE 35: The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field to mseccfg.  
RULE 36: When the MLPE field in mseccfg is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in Machine mode.  
RULE 37: When the MLPE field in mseccfg is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in Machine mode, the hart does not update the ELP state (it remains NO_LP_EXPECTED), and the LPAD instruction operates as a no‑op.  
RULE 38: When XLEN = 32, mseccfgh is a 32‑bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.  
RULE 39: The mseccfgh register exists only when XLEN = 32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN = 64.
======================================================= END - 4 ========================================================

The following description:

- The extension introduces the **RLB**, **MMWP**, and **MML** fields in the **mseccfg CSR**.  
- The physical memory protection rules that depend on **mseccfg.MML** are applied **when mseccfg.MML is set to 1**; these rules are summarized in the truth table below.

| Bits on pmpcfg L | Bits on pmpcfg R | Bits on pmpcfg W | Bits on pmpcfg X | Result in MMode (when mseccfg.MML = 1) | Result in S/U Mode (when mseccfg.MML = 1) |
|------------------|------------------|------------------|------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) |
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 1 | Access Exception | Execute‑only region |
| 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read‑only on S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read‑only on S/U mode |
| 0 | 0 | 1 | 1 | Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode |
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 | Access Exception | Read‑only region |
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 1 | Access Exception | Read/Execute region |
| 0 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Access Exception | Read/Write region |
| 0 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Access Exception | Read/Write/Execute region |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | 1 | Locked Execute‑only region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | 0 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.* |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | 1 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 | Locked Read‑only region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 | Locked Read/Execute region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Locked Read/Write region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.* | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.* |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mseccfg CSR introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields.  
RULE 2: Physical memory protection rules that depend on the mseccfg.MML field are applied only when the mseccfg.MML field is set to 1.  
RULE 3: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 0, 0, and 0 respectively, the region is inaccessible and an Access Exception is generated in both M‑mode and S/U mode.  
RULE 4: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, X=1, an Access Exception is generated in M‑mode and the region is Execute‑only in S/U mode.  
RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, X=0, the region is a shared data region that permits read/write access in M‑mode and read‑only access in S/U mode.  
RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, X=1, the region is a shared data region that permits read/write access in both M‑mode and S/U mode.  
RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, X=0, an Access Exception is generated in M‑mode and the region is read‑only in S/U mode.  
RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, X=1, an Access Exception is generated in M‑mode and the region is read/execute in S/U mode.  
RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, X=0, an Access Exception is generated in M‑mode and the region is read/write in S/U mode.  
RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, X=1, an Access Exception is generated in M‑mode and the region is read/write/execute in S/U mode.  
RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, X=0, the region is a locked inaccessible region that generates an Access Exception in both M‑mode and S/U mode.  
RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, X=1, the region is a locked Execute‑only region that generates an Access Exception in S/U mode and a locked Execute‑only region in M‑mode.  
RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, X=0, the region is a locked shared code region that permits execute‑only access in both M‑mode and S/U mode.  
RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, X=1, the region is a locked shared code region that permits execute‑only access in S/U mode and read/execute access in M‑mode.  
RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, X=0, the region is a locked read‑only region that generates an Access Exception in S/U mode and is read‑only in M‑mode.  
RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, X=1, the region is a locked read/execute region that generates an Access Exception in S/U mode and is read/execute in M‑mode.  
RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=0, the region is a locked read/write region that generates an Access Exception in S/U mode and is read/write in M‑mode.  
RULE 18: When mseccfg.MML is 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=1, the region is a locked shared data region that is read‑only in both M‑mode and S/U mode.

The following description:

- **When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in *mstatus* is set and *WRS.NTO* is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and it does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, the *WRS.NTO* instruction will cause an illegal‑instruction exception.**

- **When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit is set in *hstatus*, the TW bit in *mstatus* is clear, and the *WRS.NTO* does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, the *WRS.NTO* instruction will cause a virtual‑instruction exception.**

- **WRS.NTO, unlike WFI, is not specified to cause an illegal‑instruction exception if executed in U‑mode when the governing TW bit in *mstatus* is 0.**
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When the TW bit in mstatus is set, WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and the instruction does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 2: When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit is set in hstatus, the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and the WRS.NTO instruction does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: WRS.NTO is not specified to cause an illegal‑instruction exception when executed in U‑mode while the TW bit in mstatus is 0.

The following description:

- The VS field is a vector context status field located in **mstatus[10:9]** and is shadowed in **sstatus[10:9]**.  
- VS is defined analogously to the floating‑point context status field, **FS**.  
- **Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal‑instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.**  
- **When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.**  
- **Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.**  
- **If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.**  
- **Implementations may have a writable misa.V field.**  
- **Analogous to the way in which the floating‑point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.**  
- **Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.**
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The VS field is a vector context status field located in bits 10 down to 9 of mstatus and is shadowed in bits 10 down to 9 of sstatus.  
RULE 2: The VS field in mstatus is defined analogously to the floating‑point context status field FS.  
RULE 3: Attempts to execute any vector instruction or to access vector CSRs raise an illegal‑instruction exception when mstatus.VS is Off.  
RULE 4: When mstatus.VS is Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including vector CSRs, changes mstatus.VS to Dirty.  
RULE 5: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, then mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set according to existing specifications.  
RULE 6: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.

The following description:

- When **V=1**, attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, **raise an illegal‑instruction exception** when **either** `vsstatus.VS` **or** `mstatus.VS` is **set to Off**.

- When **V=1** and **neither** `vsstatus.VS` **nor** `mstatus.VS` **is set to Off**, executing any instruction that changes vector state, **including the vector CSRs**, **will change both** `mstatus.VS` **and** `vsstatus.VS` **to Dirty**.

- Implementations **may** change `mstatus.VS` **from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state**.

- **If** `mstatus.VS` **is Dirty, then** `mstatus.SD` **is 1; otherwise,** `mstatus.SD` **is set in accordance with existing specifications**.

RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 2: When V=1 and both vsstatus.VS and mstatus.VS are not Off, execution of any instruction that changes vector state, including accesses to vector CSRs, changes both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty.  
RULE 3: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, then mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set according to the existing specifications.

The following description:

- If the floating‑point unit status field **mstatus.FS** is **Off** then any attempt to execute a vector floating‑point instruction will raise an illegal‑instruction exception.

The following description:

- The **mstatus** register **is an MXLEN‑bit read/write register** formatted as shown in Figure 7 for RV32 and Figure 8 for RV64.  
- The **mstatus** register **keeps track of and controls the hart's current operating state**.  
- A **restricted view of mstatus appears as the sstatus register in the S‑level ISA**.  

**RV32‑only additional register**  
- For RV32 only, **mstatush is a 32‑bit read/write register** formatted as shown in Figure 9.  
- **Bits 30:4 of mstatush generally contain the same fields found in bits 62:36 of mstatus for RV64**.  
- **Fields SD, SXL, and UXL do not exist in mstatush**.

The following description:

- Global interrupt‑enable bits, **MIE** and **SIE**, are provided for M‑mode and S‑mode respectively.  
- The global **xIE** bits are located in the low‑order bits of **mstatus**, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.  
- When a hart is executing in privilege mode **x**, interrupts are globally enabled when **x IE=1** and globally disabled when **x IE=0**.  
- Interrupts for lower‑privilege modes, **w < x**, are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global **w IE** bit for the lower‑privilege mode.  
- Interrupts for higher‑privilege modes, **y > x**, are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global **y IE** bit for the higher‑privilege mode.  
- If supervisor mode is not implemented, then **SIE** and **SPIE** are read‑only 0.  
- When a trap is taken from privilege mode **y** into privilege mode **x**, **x PIE** is set to the value of **x IE**; **x IE** is set to 0; and **x PP** is set to **y**.  
- The **x PP** fields can only hold privilege modes up to **x**, so **MPP** is two bits wide and **SPP** is one bit wide.  
- An **MRET** or **SRET** instruction is used to return from a trap in M‑mode or S‑mode respectively.  
- When executing an **x RET** instruction, supposing **x PP** holds the value **y**, **x IE** is set to **x PIE**; the privilege mode is changed to **y**; **x PIE** is set to 1; and **x PP** is set to the least‑privileged supported mode (U if U‑mode is implemented, else M). If **y ≠ M**, **x RET** also sets **MPRV=0**.  
- **x PP** fields are **WARL** fields that can hold only privilege mode **x** and any implemented privilege mode lower than **x**.  
- If privilege mode **x** is not implemented, then **x PP** must be read‑only 0.  
- M‑mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to **MPP** then reading it back.  
- If the machine provides only U and M modes, then only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in **MPP**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MIE bit in mstatus provides the global interrupt enable for M‑mode, and the SIE bit in mstatus provides the global interrupt enable for S‑mode.  
RULE 2: The MIE and SIE bits reside in the low‑order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.  
RULE 3: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled if the xIE bit in mstatus is 1 and globally disabled if that bit is 0.  
RULE 4: Interrupts for any privilege mode w that is lower than the current mode x are always globally disabled, irrespective of the wIE bit value in mstatus.  
RULE 5: Interrupts for any privilege mode y that is higher than the current mode x are always globally enabled, irrespective of the yIE bit value in mstatus.  
RULE 6: If supervisor mode is not implemented, the SIE and SPIE bits in mstatus are read‑only 0.  
RULE 7: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y to privilege mode x, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to the current value of the xIE bit, the xIE bit is cleared to 0, and the xPP field is written with the value y.  
RULE 8: The MPP field in mstatus is two bits wide and can hold only privilege modes up to M, while the SPP field in mstatus is one bit wide and can hold only privilege modes up to S.  
RULE 9: Executing an xRET instruction causes the xIE bit in mstatus to be set to the value of xPIE, changes the current privilege mode to the value held in xPP, sets xPIE to 1, writes the least‑privileged supported mode into xPP, and, if the target mode y is not M, also clears the MPRV bit in mstatus.  
RULE 10: Each xPP field in mstatus is a WARL field that may contain only privilege mode x or any implemented privilege mode lower than x.  
RULE 11: If privilege mode x is not implemented, the corresponding xPP field in mstatus must be read‑only 0.  
RULE 12: Machine‑mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode into the MPP field of mstatus and then reading it back.  
RULE 13: When the implementation provides only U and M privilege modes, the MPP field of mstatus requires only a single hardware storage bit to represent both 00 and 11.

The following description:

- Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1.  

- When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0.  

- For RV64, this clearing occurs regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.  

- The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write.  

- For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit in mstatus register.  

- When a trap is to be taken into M‑mode, if the MDT bit is currently 0, it is then set to 1, and the trap is delivered as expected.  

- However, if MDT is already set to 1, then this is an unexpected trap.  

- When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.  

- A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit.  

- The MRET and SRET instructions, when executed in M‑mode, set the MDT bit to 0.  

- If the new privilege mode is U, VS, or VU, then sstatus.SDT is also set to 0.  

- Additionally, if it is VU, then vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.  

- The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.  

- If it is U, VS, or VU, then sstatus.SDT is also set to 0.  

- Additionally, if it is VU, then vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Upon reset, the mstatus register's MDT field is set to 1.  
RULE 2: When the MDT bit in mstatus is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE bit in mstatus is cleared to 0.  
RULE 3: For RV64, this clearing of the MIE bit in mstatus occurs regardless of any value written to MIE by the same CSR write.  
RULE 4: The MIE bit in mstatus can be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write only if the MDT bit in mstatus is already 0, or for RV64 only if the MDT bit is being set to 0 by the same write.  
RULE 5: For RV32, the MDT field resides in the mstatush register while the MIE bit resides in the mstatus register.  
RULE 6: When a trap is taken into M‑mode and the MDT bit in mstatus is 0, the MDT bit in mstatus is set to 1 before the trap is delivered.  
RULE 7: If the MDT bit in mstatus is already 1 when a trap is taken into M‑mode, the trap is considered an unexpected trap.  
RULE 8: When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the MDT bit value in mstatus.  
RULE 9: A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit in mstatus.  
RULE 10: The MRET instruction, when executed in M‑mode, sets the MDT bit in mstatus to 0.  
RULE 11: The SRET instruction, when executed in M‑mode, sets the MDT bit in mstatus to 0.  
RULE 12: If the new privilege mode after executing MRET or SRET is U, VS, or VU, the sstatus.SDT field is also set to 0 and the mstatus register's MDT bit has already been set to 0 by that instruction.  
RULE 13: If the new privilege mode after executing MRET or SRET is VU, the vsstatus.SDT field is also set to 0 and the mstatus register's MDT bit has already been set to 0 by that instruction.  
RULE 14: The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit in mstatus to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.  
RULE 15: If the new privilege mode after executing MNRET is U, VS, or VU, the sstatus.SDT field is also set to 0 and the mstatus register's MDT bit has been set to 0 by that instruction.  
RULE 16: If the new privilege mode after executing MNRET is VU, the vsstatus.SDT field is also set to 0 and the mstatus register's MDT bit has been set to 0 by that instruction.

The following description:

- For RV64 harts, the **SXL** and **UXL** fields in the **mstatus** register are **WARL** fields that control the value of **XLEN** for **S‑mode** and **U‑mode**, respectively.  
- The encoding of the **SXL** and **UXL** fields is the same as the **MXL** field of the **misa** register (see Table 11).  

- **When MXLEN = 32**:  
  - The **SXL** and **UXL** fields **do not exist**.  
  - **SXLEN = 32** and **UXLEN = 32**.  

- **When MXLEN = 64**:  
  - **If S‑mode is not supported**, **SXL** is **read‑only zero**.  
  - **If S‑mode is supported**, **SXL** is a **WARL** field that **encodes the current value of SXLEN**.  
    - *In particular*, an implementation **may make SXL be a read‑only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN = MXLEN**.  

  - **If U‑mode is not supported**, **UXL** is **read‑only zero**.  
  - **If U‑mode is supported**, **UXL** is a **WARL** field that **encodes the current value of UXLEN**.  
    - *In particular*, an implementation **may make UXL be a read‑only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN = MXLEN** or **UXLEN = SXLEN**.  

- **If S‑mode is implemented**, the **set of legal values** that the **UXL** field may assume **excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN**.

RULE 8: When MXLEN = 64 and U‑mode is supported, UXL is a WARL field in the mstatus register that encodes the current value of UXLEN.

The following description:

- The MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.  
- When MPRV = 0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.  
- When MPRV = 1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.  
- Instruction address‑translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV.  
- MPRV is read‑only 0 if U‑mode is not supported.  
- An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV = 0.  
- The MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.  
- When MXR = 0, only loads from pages marked readable (R = 1 in Figure 67) will succeed.  
- When MXR = 1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R = 1 or X = 1) will succeed.  
- MXR has no effect when page‑based virtual memory is not in effect.  
- MXR is read‑only 0 if S‑mode is not supported.  
- When MPRV = 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.  
- The SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit modifies the privilege with which S‑mode loads and stores access virtual memory.  
- When SUM = 0, S‑mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U‑mode (U = 1 in Figure 67) will fault.  
- When SUM = 1, these accesses are permitted.  
- SUM has no effect when page‑based virtual memory is not in effect.  
- While SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S‑mode, it is in effect when MPRV = 1 and MPP = S.  
- SUM is read‑only 0 if S‑mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read‑only 0.  
- The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page‑table entries.  
- In particular, they have no impact on whether access‑fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MPRV bit in mstatus modifies the effective privilege mode for explicit memory accesses.  
RULE 2: When mstatus.MPRV = 0, explicit memory accesses use the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.  
RULE 3: When mstatus.MPRV = 1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied as though the current privilege mode were set to the value in the MPP field of mstatus.  
RULE 4: Instruction address‑translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of mstatus.MPRV.  
RULE 5: mstatus.MPRV is read‑only 0 if U‑mode is not supported.  
RULE 6: An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets mstatus.MPRV = 0.  
RULE 7: The MXR bit in mstatus modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.  
RULE 8: When mstatus.MXR = 0, only loads from pages marked readable (R = 1) will succeed.  
RULE 9: When mstatus.MXR = 1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R = 1 or X = 1) will succeed.  
RULE 10: mstatus.MXR has no effect when page‑based virtual memory is not in effect.  
RULE 11: mstatus.MXR is read‑only 0 if S‑mode is not supported.  
RULE 12: When mstatus.MPRV = 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to the XLEN of the privilege level indicated by the MPP field of mstatus, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.  
RULE 13: The SUM bit in mstatus modifies the privilege with which S‑mode loads and stores access virtual memory.  
RULE 14: When mstatus.SUM = 0, S‑mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U‑mode (U = 1) will fault.  
RULE 15: When mstatus.SUM = 1, S‑mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U‑mode are permitted.  
RULE 16: mstatus.SUM has no effect when page‑based virtual memory is not in effect.  
RULE 17: While mstatus.SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S‑mode, it is in effect when mstatus.MPRV = 1 and mstatus.MPP = S.  
RULE 18: mstatus.SUM is read‑only 0 if S‑mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read‑only 0.  
RULE 19: The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page‑table entries.  
RULE 20: The MXR and SUM mechanisms have no impact on whether access‑fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

The following description:

- The **MBE**, **SBE**, and **UBE** bits in **mstatus** and **mstatush** are **WARL** fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches. **Instruction fetches are always little‑endian**.

- **MBE** controls whether non‑instruction‑fetch memory accesses made from **M‑mode** (assuming **mstatus.MPRV = 0**) are little‑endian (**MBE = 0**) or big‑endian (**MBE = 1**).

- **If S‑mode is not supported**, **SBE** is **read‑only 0**.  
  **Otherwise**, **SBE** controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from **S‑mode** are little‑endian (**SBE = 0**) or big‑endian (**SBE = 1**).

- **If U‑mode is not supported**, **UBE** is **read‑only 0**.  
  **Otherwise**, **UBE** controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from **U‑mode** are little‑endian (**UBE = 0**) or big‑endian (**UBE = 1**).

- For **implicit accesses** to supervisor‑level memory‑management data structures (e.g., page tables), endianness is **always controlled by SBE**.  
  **Since changing SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of these data structures, if any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M‑mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an **`SFENCE.VMA`** instruction with **rs1 = x0** and **rs2 = x0**.**

- In the usual case where **SBE** is changed only on a world switch, **no additional `SFENCE.VMA` is necessary**, beyond what would ordinarily be required for a world switch.

- **If S‑mode is supported**, an implementation **may make SBE be a read‑only copy of MBE**.

- **If U‑mode is supported**, an implementation **may make UBE be a read‑only copy of either MBE or SBE**.

- An implementation **supports only little‑endian memory accesses** if fields **MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read‑only 0**.

- An implementation **supports only big‑endian memory accesses** (aside from instruction fetches) if **MBE is read‑only 1** and **SBE and UBE are each read‑only 1** when **S‑mode and U‑mode are supported**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.  
RULE 2: Instruction fetches are always little‑endian.  
RULE 3: The MBE bit in mstatus controls whether non‑instruction‑fetch memory accesses made from M‑mode (assuming mstatus.MPRV = 0) are little‑endian when MBE = 0 and big‑endian when MBE = 1.  
RULE 4: When S‑mode is not supported, the SBE bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.  
RULE 5: When S‑mode is supported, the SBE bit in mstatus controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S‑mode are little‑endian when SBE = 0 and big‑endian when SBE = 1.  
RULE 6: When U‑mode is not supported, the UBE bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.  
RULE 7: When U‑mode is supported, the UBE bit in mstatus controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U‑mode are little‑endian when UBE = 0 and big‑endian when UBE = 1.  
RULE 8: Implicit accesses to supervisor‑level memory‑management data structures, such as page tables, always have their endianness controlled by the SBE bit in mstatus.  
RULE 9: If supervisor‑level memory‑management data structures remain in use across a change to the SBE bit in mstatus, M‑mode software must follow the SBE change by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.  
RULE 10: When SBE is changed only on a world switch, no additional SFENCE.VMA instruction beyond the usual world‑switch requirement is necessary.  
RULE 11: An implementation supports only little‑endian memory accesses if the MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus are all read‑only 0.  
RULE 12: An implementation supports only big‑endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if the MBE bit in mstatus is read‑only 1 and, when S‑mode and U‑mode are supported, the SBE and UBE bits in mstatus are each read‑only 1.

The following description:

- **TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit in mstatus** – a WARL field.  
  - *Condition:* TVM = 1 and the processor is executing in S‑mode.  
    *Effect:* Attempts to read or write the **satp** CSR, or to execute an **SFENCE.VMA** or **SINVAL.VMA** instruction **raise an illegal‑instruction exception**.  
  - *Condition:* TVM = 0 and the processor is executing in S‑mode.  
    *Effect:* The above operations (**satp** CSR accesses, **SFENCE.VMA**, **SINVAL.VMA**) are **permitted**.  
  - *Condition:* S‑mode is not supported.  
    *Effect:* TVM is **read‑only 0**.

- **TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus** – a WARL field.  
  - *Condition:* TW = 0.  
    *Effect:* The **WFI** instruction **may execute** in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.  
  - *Condition:* TW = 1 and **WFI** is executed in any mode less privileged than M and **does not complete within an implementation‑specific, bounded time limit**.  
    *Effect:* The **WFI** instruction **causes an illegal‑instruction exception**.  
  - *Additional implementation note:* An implementation may have **WFI** always raise an illegal‑instruction exception in modes less privileged than M when TW = 1, even if there are pending globally‑disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.  
  - *Condition:* There are **no modes less privileged than M**.  
    *Effect:* TW is **read‑only 0**.  
  - *Condition:* S‑mode is implemented and **WFI** is executed in U‑mode.  
    *Effect:* **Illegal‑instruction exception** is raised **regardless of the value of TW**, unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific, bounded time limit.

- **TSR (Trap SRET) bit in mstatus** – a WARL field.  
  - *Condition:* TSR = 1 and the processor is executing in S‑mode.  
    *Effect:* Attempts to execute **SRET** **raise an illegal‑instruction exception**.  
  - *Condition:* TSR = 0 and the processor is executing in S‑mode.  
    *Effect:* Executing **SRET** is **permitted**.  
  - *Condition:* S‑mode is not supported.  
    *Effect:* TSR is **read‑only 0**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The TVM field in mstatus is a WARL field.  
RULE 2: When TVM in mstatus is 1 and the processor is executing in S-mode, attempts to read or write the satp CSR, or to execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: When TVM in mstatus is 0 and the processor is executing in S-mode, accesses to the satp CSR and execution of SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instructions are permitted.  
RULE 4: When S-mode is not supported, the TVM bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.  
RULE 5: The TW field in mstatus is a WARL field.  
RULE 6: When TW in mstatus is 0, the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M provided it is not prevented for another reason.  
RULE 7: When TW in mstatus is 1 and the WFI instruction is executed in any mode less privileged than M and does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, the WFI instruction causes an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 8: An implementation may cause the WFI instruction to always raise an illegal‑instruction exception in modes less privileged than M when TW in mstatus is 1, even if globally‑disabled interrupts are pending at execution time.  
RULE 9: When no modes less privileged than M exist, the TW bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.  
RULE 10: When S-mode is implemented and the WFI instruction is executed in U-mode, an illegal‑instruction exception is raised regardless of the value of TW unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit.  
RULE 11: The TSR field in mstatus is a WARL field.  
RULE 12: When TSR in mstatus is 1 and the processor is executing in S-mode, attempts to execute the SRET instruction raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 13: When TSR in mstatus is 0 and the processor is executing in S-mode, execution of the SRET instruction is permitted.  
RULE 14: When S-mode is not supported, the TSR bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.

The following description:

- The **FS[1:0]** and **VS[1:0]** fields in **mstatus** are *WARL* fields; the **XS[1:0]** field in **mstatus** is *read‑only*.  
- **FS** encodes the status of the floating‑point unit state, including the floating‑point registers **f0‑f31** and the CSRs **fcsr**, **frm**, and **fflags**.  
- **VS** encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers **v0‑v31** and the CSRs **vcsr**, **vxrm**, **vxsat**, **vstart**, **vl**, **vtype**, and **vlenb**.  
- **XS** encodes the status of additional user‑mode extensions and associated state.  

- The **FS**, **VS**, and **XS** fields use the status encoding shown in Table 13, with the four possible values:  

  | Status value | Meaning (FS/VS) | Meaning (XS) |
  |---------------|-----------------|--------------|
  | 0 | Off | All off |
  | 1 | Initial | None dirty or clean, some on |
  | 2 | Clean | None dirty, some clean |
  | 3 | Dirty | Some dirty |

- **If the F extension is implemented, the FS field shall not be read‑only zero.**  
- **If neither the F extension nor S‑mode is implemented, then FS is read‑only zero.**  
- **If S‑mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS may optionally be read‑only zero.**  
- Implementations with S‑mode but without the F extension are *permitted* (but not required) to make the FS field be read‑only zero; some such implementations may choose *not* to make FS read‑only zero in order to enable emulation of the F extension via invisible traps into M‑mode.  

- **If the v registers are implemented, the VS field shall not be read‑only zero.**  
- **If neither the v registers nor S‑mode is implemented, then VS is read‑only zero.**  
- **If S‑mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS may optionally be read‑only zero.**  

- **In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field is read‑only zero.**  
- The **XS** field *effectively* reports the *maximum* status value across all user‑extension status fields (individual extensions may use a different encoding).  

- The **SD** bit in **mstatus** is a *read‑only* bit that *summarizes* whether either the **FS**, **VS**, or **XS** fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory.  
- **If FS, XS, and VS are all read‑only zero, then SD is also always zero.**  

- **When an extension's status is set to Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal‑instruction exception.**  
- **When the status is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.**  
- **When the status is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.**  
- **When the status is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.**  

- **During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean.**  
- **During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean (it should never be Dirty at restore).**  
- **If the status is Initial, the context must be set to an initial constant value on context restore to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory.**  

- **The FS and XS fields are read by the privileged code before saving the context.**  
- **The FS field is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context, while the XS field is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions.**  
- **The status fields will also be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode.**  

- **Executing a user‑mode instruction to disable a unit and place it into the Off state will cause an illegal‑instruction exception to be raised if any subsequent instruction tries to use the unit before it is turned back on.**  
- **Changing the setting of FS has no effect on the contents of the floating‑point register state. In particular, setting FS=Off does not destroy the state, nor does setting FS=Initial clear the contents.**  
- **Similarly, the setting of VS has no effect on the contents of the vector register state.**  

- Implementations **may choose** to track the dirtiness of the floating‑point register state *imprecisely* by reporting the state to be Dirty even when it has not been modified.  
- **On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate the floating‑point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty.**  
- **On other implementations, dirtiness might not be tracked at all, in which case the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty.**  
- **This definition of FS does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation. Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to close a potential side channel.**  
- **If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating‑point register or the fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it is implementation‑defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.**  

- Implementations **may choose** to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean.  
- **When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it is implementation‑defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.**  

- **The SD bit is read‑only and is set when either the FS, VS, or XS bits encode a Dirty state (i.e., SD = (FS==0b11 OR XS==0b11 OR VS==0b11)).**  

- **Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits.**  
- **Supervisor‑level software normally uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor‑level saved context. Machine‑level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring the extension state in their corresponding version of the context.**  

### Table 14 – FS, VS, and XS State Transitions  

| Current state | Action | Condition | Architectural effect / Next state |
|---------------|--------|-----------|-----------------------------------|
| Off | At context save in privileged code | — | No state saved; next state remains **Off** |
| Initial | At context save in privileged code | — | No state saved; next state remains **Initial** |
| Clean | At context save in privileged code | — | No state saved; next state remains **Clean** |
| Dirty | At context save in privileged code | — | State saved; next state becomes **Clean** |
| Off | At context restore in privileged code | — | No state restored; next state remains **Off** |
| Initial | At context restore in privileged code | — | State restored to the initial constant; next state remains **Initial** |
| Clean | At context restore in privileged code | — | State restored from memory; next state remains **Clean** |
| Dirty | At context restore in privileged code | — | *N/A* (no restore action defined) |
| Off | Execute instruction to read state | — | Illegal‑instruction exception |
| Initial | Execute instruction to read state | — | Normal execution (no exception) |
| Clean | Execute instruction to read state | — | Normal execution (no exception) |
| Dirty | Execute instruction to read state | — | Normal execution (no exception) |
| Off | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state (including configuration) | — | Illegal‑instruction exception |
| Initial | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state (including configuration) | — | Transition to **Dirty** (execute dirty) |
| Clean | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state (including configuration) | — | Transition to **Dirty** (execute dirty) |
| Dirty | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state (including configuration) | — | Remains **Dirty** (execute dirty) |
| Off | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | — | Illegal‑instruction exception |
| Initial | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |
| Clean | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |
| Dirty | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |
| Off | Execute instruction to disable unit | — | Transition to **Off** |
| Initial | Execute instruction to disable unit | — | Transition to **Off** |
| Clean | Execute instruction to disable unit | — | Transition to **Off** |
| Dirty | Execute instruction to disable unit | — | Transition to **Off** |
| Off | Execute instruction to enable unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |
| Initial | Execute instruction to enable unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |
| Clean | Execute instruction to enable unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |
| Dirty | Execute instruction to enable unit | — | Transition to **Initial** |

*(Each row applies independently to the FS, VS, or XS status bits in **mstatus**.)*

- The Zicfilp extension adds the **SPELP** and **MPELP** fields in the **mstatus** register.

RULE 4: The MPELP field in mstatus holds the previous Expected Landing Pad state.  
RULE 5: The SPELP field in mstatus is updated as specified in Section 23.1.2.  
RULE 6: The MPELP field in mstatus is updated as specified in Section 23.1.2.

The following description:

- When a trap is delegated to S‑mode, the **SPP field of mstatus** *is written* with the active privilege mode at the time of the trap.  

- When a trap is delegated to S‑mode, the **SPIE field of mstatus** *is written* with the value of the SIE field at the time of the trap.

RULE 3: When a trap is delegated to S-mode, the SIE field of mstatus is cleared.  
RULE 4: When a trap is delegated to S-mode, the MPP field of mstatus is not written.  
RULE 5: When a trap is delegated to S-mode, the MPIE field of mstatus is not written.

The following description:

- An interrupt **i** will trap to M‑mode (causing the privilege mode to change to M‑mode) **if all of the following are true**:  
  (a) **either** the current privilege mode is M **and** the **MIE bit in the mstatus register is set**, **or** the current privilege mode has less privilege than M‑mode;  
  (b) bit i is set in both **mip** and **mie**; and  
  (c) if the register **mideleg** exists, bit i is not set in **mideleg**.  

- The conditions for an interrupt trap to occur **must be evaluated** "immediately following the execution of an x RET instruction or an explicit write to a CSR on which these interrupt trap conditions expressly depend (including **mip**, **mie**, **mstatus**, and **mideleg**)."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An interrupt i will trap to M‑mode only when the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set.  

RULE 2: Implementations must evaluate interrupt trap conditions immediately after the execution of an xRET instruction or an explicit write to any CSR on which those conditions expressly depend, including the mstatus register.

The following description:

- **TW field in mstatus**  
  - *Condition*: TW = 1 in **mstatus**.  
  - *Effect*: The **WFI** instruction **may raise** an illegal‑instruction exception (as described in Section 3.1.6.6).

- **Global interrupt enable bits in mstatus (MIE and SIE)**  
  - *Condition*: Any value of **MIE** or **SIE** (i.e., regardless of whether they are 0 or 1).  
  - *Effect*: The operation of **WFI** **must be unaffected** by those bits; the hart must resume if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending even when the global interrupt enable bits are cleared.

- **Requirement related to global interrupt enables** (derivative of the previous point)  
  - *Condition*: Locally enabled interrupts are pending at any privilege level, and the global interrupt enable bits (**MIE**, **SIE**) at each privilege level may be either 0 or 1.  
  - *Effect*: **WFI** is **required to resume execution** for those pending locally enabled interrupts, regardless of the state of the global interrupt enable bits in **mstatus**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If the TW field in mstatus is 1, execution of the WFI instruction may raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 2: The operation of the WFI instruction must be unaffected by the values of the MIE and SIE bits in mstatus.  
RULE 3: The hart must resume execution when a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending even if the MIE and SIE bits in mstatus are cleared.  
RULE 4: When locally enabled interrupts are pending at any privilege level, execution of WFI must resume for those pending interrupts regardless of the values of the MIE and SIE bits in mstatus.

The following description:

- PMP checks are applied to all accesses whose effective privilege mode is **S** or **U**; this includes instruction fetches and data accesses in **S** and **U** mode, **and data accesses in M‑mode when the **MPRV** bit in **mstatus** is set and the **MPP** field in **mstatus** contains **S** or **U**.  

- The **MPRV** bit in **mstatus**, when set, together with the **MPP** field in **mstatus** containing **S** or **U**, causes the effective privilege mode of a data access performed in M‑mode to be **S** or **U**, thereby making the access subject to PMP checks.  

- The **MPP** field in **mstatus** can contain the privilege levels **S** or **U**; when the **MPRV** bit is set, the value of **MPP** determines the effective privilege mode used for PMP checking of M‑mode data accesses.

RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 1: PMP checks are applied to all accesses whose effective privilege mode is S or U.  
RULE 2: Instruction fetches and data accesses executed in S mode are subject to PMP checks because their effective privilege mode is S.  
RULE 3: Instruction fetches and data accesses executed in U mode are subject to PMP checks because their effective privilege mode is U.  
RULE 4: Data accesses executed in M mode are subject to PMP checks when the MPRV bit in mstatus is set and the MPP field in mstatus contains S or U.  
RULE 5: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is set and the MPP field in mstatus contains S or U, the effective privilege mode of a data access performed in M mode becomes S or U.  
RULE 6: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is set and the MPP field in mstatus contains S or U, a data access performed in M mode is subject to PMP checks.  
RULE 7: The MPP field in mstatus may hold either the privilege level S or the privilege level U.  
RULE 8: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is set, the value of the MPP field in mstatus determines the effective privilege mode used for PMP checking of data accesses performed in M mode.

The following description:

- The **mstatus** register contains a field named **SPP** that "indicates the privilege level at which a hart was executing before entering supervisor mode."  
  - When a trap is taken, **SPP** is set to 0 if the trap originated from user mode, or 1 otherwise.  
  - When an **SRET** instruction is executed to return from the trap handler, the privilege level is set to user mode if the **SPP** bit is 0, or supervisor mode if the **SPP** bit is 1; **SPP** is then set to 0.

- The **mstatus** register contains a field named **SIE** that "enables or disables all interrupts in supervisor mode."  
  - When **SIE** is clear, interrupts are not taken while in supervisor mode.  
  - When the hart is running in user mode, the value in **SIE** is ignored, and supervisor‑level interrupts are enabled.

- The **mstatus** register contains a field named **SPIE** that "indicates whether supervisor interrupts were enabled prior to trapping into supervisor mode."  
  - When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, **SPIE** is set to the current value of **SIE**, and **SIE** is set to 0.  
  - When an **SRET** instruction is executed, **SIE** is set to the value of **SPIE**, then **SPIE** is set to 1.

- The **sstatus** register "is a subset of the **mstatus** register."

- "In a straightforward implementation, reading or writing any field in **sstatus** is equivalent to reading or writing the homonymous field in **mstatus**."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register contains a field named SPP that indicates the privilege level at which a hart was executing before entering supervisor mode.  
RULE 2: When a trap is taken, the SPP field in mstatus is set to 0 if the trap originated from user mode.  
RULE 3: When a trap is taken, the SPP field in mstatus is set to 1 if the trap did not originate from user mode.  
RULE 4: When an SRET instruction is executed and the SPP bit in mstatus is 0, the privilege level is set to user mode.  
RULE 5: When an SRET instruction is executed and the SPP bit in mstatus is 1, the privilege level is set to supervisor mode.  
RULE 6: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPP field in mstatus is set to 0.  
RULE 7: The mstatus register contains a field named SIE that enables or disables all interrupts in supervisor mode.  
RULE 8: When the SIE field in mstatus is clear, interrupts are not taken while the hart is in supervisor mode.  
RULE 9: When the hart is running in user mode, the value in the SIE field of mstatus is ignored.  
RULE 10: When the hart is running in user mode, supervisor‑level interrupts are enabled regardless of the value of the SIE field in mstatus.  
RULE 11: The mstatus register contains a field named SPIE that indicates whether supervisor interrupts were enabled prior to trapping into supervisor mode.  
RULE 12: When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, the SPIE field in mstatus is set to the current value of the SIE field in mstatus.  
RULE 13: When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, the SIE field in mstatus is set to 0.  
RULE 14: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SIE field in mstatus is set to the value of the SPIE field in mstatus.  
RULE 15: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPIE field in mstatus is set to 1.  
RULE 16: All fields that appear in the sstatus register also appear in the mstatus register.  
RULE 17: In a straightforward implementation, reading or writing any field in sstatus is equivalent to reading or writing the homonymous field in mstatus.
======================================================= END - 34 =======================================================

The following description:

- Step 6: The requested memory access is allowed by the pte.u bit given the current privilege mode and the value of the SUM and MXR fields of the mstatus register. If not, stop and raise a page‑fault exception corresponding to the original access type.

RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

The following description:

- An attempt to execute **SINVAL.VMA** or **HINVAL.GVMA** in **S-mode** or **HS-mode** **when mstatus .TVM=1** also raises an illegal‑instruction exception.

- **SFENCE.W.INVAL** and **SFENCE.INVAL.IR** are **unaffected by the mstatus .TVM** field and hence are always permitted in **S-mode** and **VS-mode**.

=========

RULE 2: The SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions are always permitted in S-mode and VS-mode regardless of the value of the mstatus.TVM field.  
RULE 3: When the mstatus.TVM field is 1, the SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions do not need to be trapped because they have only ordering effects and no visible side effects.

The following description:

- The hstatus fields VTSR, VTW, and VTVM are defined analogously to the mstatus fields TSR, TW, and TVM, but affect execution only in VS-mode, and cause virtual‑instruction exceptions instead of illegal‑instruction exceptions.

- When VTW=1 (and assuming mstatus .TW=0), an attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual‑instruction exception if the WFI does not complete within an implementation‑specific, bounded time limit.

- An implementation may have WFI always raise a virtual‑instruction exception in VS-mode when VTW=1 (and mstatus .TW=0), even if there are pending globally‑disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus field TSR causes an illegal‑instruction exception if its restriction is violated.  
RULE 2: The mstatus field TW causes an illegal‑instruction exception if its restriction is violated.  
RULE 3: The mstatus field TVM causes an illegal‑instruction exception if its restriction is violated.  
RULE 4: If mstatus.TW is 0 and the hstatus field VTW is 1, then an attempt in VS-mode to execute a WFI instruction raises a virtual‑instruction exception when the WFI does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit.

The following description:

- The hypervisor extension **adds two fields, MPV and GVA, to the machine‑level `mstatus` CSR** (or to `mstatush` when `MXLEN=32`).  
  - *When `MXLEN=64`* the modified `mstatus` register contains MPV and GVA.  
  - *When `MXLEN=32`* the hypervisor extension **adds MPV and GVA not to `mstatus` but to `mstatush`**; the format of `mstatus` is unchanged for RV32.

- **MPV (Machine Previous Virtualization Mode) bit**  
  - *Written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.*  
  - "Just as the MPP field is set to the (nominal) privilege mode at the time of the trap, the MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap."  
  - *When an `MRET` instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, **unless MPP=3**, in which case V remains 0.*

- **GVA (Guest Virtual Address) field**  
  - *Written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.*  
  - *For any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest‑page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to `mtval`, GVA is set to 1.*  
  - *For any other trap into M‑mode, GVA is set to 0.*

- **TSR and TVM fields of `mstatus`**  
  - *Affect execution only in HS‑mode, not in VS‑mode.*

- **TW field of `mstatus`**  
  - *Affects execution in all modes except M‑mode.*

- **Effect of TVM=1** (TVM is a field in `mstatus`)  
  - *Prevents HS‑mode from accessing `hgatp` or executing `HFENCE.GVMA` or `HINVAL.GVMA`.*  
  - *Has no effect on accesses to `vsatp` or instructions `HFENCE.VVMA` or `HINVAL.VVMA`.*  
  - *Setting TVM=1 does **not** cause traps for accesses to `vsatp` or instructions `HFENCE.VVMA` or `HINVAL.VVMA`, nor for any actions taken in VS‑mode.*

- **MPRV (Modify Privilege) field of `mstatus`** (behavior changed by the hypervisor extension)  
  - *When `MPRV=0`, translation and protection **behave as normal**; the current privilege mode applies.*  
  - *When `MPRV=1`, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, **as though** the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.*  

  Table 49 – Effect of `MPRV` on the translation and protection of explicit memory accesses  

  | MPRV | MPV | MPP | Effect (as stated) |
  |-----|-----|-----|----------------------|
  | 0   | –   | –   | Normal access; current privilege mode applies. |
  | 1   | 0   | 0   | U‑level access with HS‑level translation and protection only. |
  | 1   | 0   | 1   | HS‑level access with HS‑level translation and protection only. |
  | 1   | –   | 3   | M‑level access with no translation. |
  | 1   | 1   | 0   | VU‑level access with two‑stage translation and protection. The HS‑level `MXR` bit makes any executable page readable. `vsstatus.MXR` makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest‑physical translation stage. |
  | 1   | 1   | 1   | VS‑level access with two‑stage translation and protection. The HS‑level `MXR` bit makes any executable page readable. `vsstatus.MXR` makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest‑physical translation stage. `vsstatus.SUM` applies instead of the HS‑level `SUM` bit. |

- **MPRV does not affect the virtual‑machine load/store instructions `HLV`, `HLVX`, and `HSV`.**  
  - *The explicit loads and stores of these instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were `hstatus.SPVP`, overriding `MPRV`.*

- **Relationship to other status registers**  
  - *The `mstatus` register is a superset of the HS‑level `sstatus` register but is **not** a superset of `vsstatus`.*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to the machine‑level mstatus CSR (or to mstatush when MXLEN=32).  
RULE 2: When MXLEN=64 the modified mstatus register contains the MPV and GVA fields.  
RULE 3: When MXLEN=32 the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA to mstatush rather than to mstatus, leaving the format of mstatus unchanged for RV32.  
RULE 4: The MPV bit in mstatus is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.  
RULE 5: At the time a trap is taken into M‑mode, the MPV bit in mstatus is set to the value of the virtualization mode V.  
RULE 6: When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to the value of the MPV bit in mstatus unless the MPP field in mstatus equals 3, in which case V remains 0.  
RULE 7: The GVA field in mstatus is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.  
RULE 8: For any trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, the GVA field in mstatus is set to 1.  
RULE 9: For any trap into M‑mode that does not write a guest virtual address to mtval, the GVA field in mstatus is set to 0.  
RULE 10: The TSR field of mstatus affects execution only in HS‑mode and has no effect in VS‑mode.  
RULE 11: The TVM field of mstatus affects execution only in HS‑mode and has no effect in VS‑mode.  
RULE 12: The TW field of mstatus affects execution in all modes except M‑mode.  
RULE 13: When the TVM field in mstatus is set to 1, HS‑mode is prevented from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.  
RULE 14: When the TVM field in mstatus is set to 1, accesses to vsatp and the execution of HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA are not affected.  
RULE 15: Setting the TVM field in mstatus to 1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp, for execution of HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, nor for any actions taken in VS‑mode.  
RULE 16: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 0, translation and protection behave as normal and the current privilege mode applies.  
RULE 17: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied as though the current virtualization mode were set to the MPV field in mstatus and the current nominal privilege mode were set to the MPP field in mstatus.  
RULE 18: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, MPV is 0, and MPP is 0, explicit memory accesses have U‑level access with HS‑level translation and protection only.  
RULE 19: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, MPV is 0, and MPP is 1, explicit memory accesses have HS‑level access with HS‑level translation and protection only.  
RULE 20: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1 and MPP is 3, explicit memory accesses have M‑level access with no translation, regardless of the MPV value.  
RULE 21: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, MPV is 1, and MPP is 0, explicit memory accesses have VU‑level access with two‑stage translation and protection; the HS‑level MXR bit makes any executable page readable and vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage only if they are readable at the guest‑physical translation stage.  
RULE 22: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, MPV is 1, and MPP is 1, explicit memory accesses have VS‑level access with two‑stage translation and protection; the HS‑level MXR bit makes any executable page readable, vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage only if they are readable at the guest‑physical translation stage, and vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS‑level SUM bit.  
RULE 23: The MPRV field in mstatus does not affect the virtual‑machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV.  
RULE 24: For the virtual‑machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV, the explicit loads and stores always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding any setting of the MPRV field in mstatus.  
RULE 25: The mstatus register is a superset of the HS‑level sstatus register.  
RULE 26: The mstatus register is not a superset of the VS‑level vsstatus register.
