The following description:

- An instruction is HS‑qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS‑mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), **assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero**.

- **Because TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S‑mode (HS‑mode), they are ignored for determining exceptions in VS‑mode.**

- **A virtual‑instruction exception is raised** for the following cases (each bullet below inherits this effect):

  - in VU‑mode, attempts to execute **WFI when mstatus .TW=0**, or to execute a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE);
  - in VS‑mode, attempts to execute **WFI when hstatus .VTW=1 and mstatus .TW=0**, unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific, bounded time;
  - in VS‑mode or VU‑mode, attempts to access an implemented non‑high‑half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS‑mode, **assuming mstatus .TVM=0**;
  - in VS‑mode or VU‑mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high‑half hypervisor CSR or high‑half VS CSR when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low‑half partner would be allowed in HS‑mode, **assuming mstatus .TVM=0**;
  - in VU‑mode, attempts to access an implemented non‑high‑half supervisor CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS‑mode, **assuming mstatus .TVM=0**;
  - in VU‑mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high‑half supervisor CSR when the same access to the CSR's low‑half partner would be allowed in HS‑mode, **assuming mstatus .TVM=0**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An instruction is HS‑qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS‑mode for some register operand values when the TSR and TVM fields of the mstatus CSR are both zero.  
RULE 2: The TSR and TVM fields of the mstatus CSR are intended to impact only S‑mode (HS‑mode) and are ignored for determining exceptions in VS‑mode.  
RULE 3: In VU‑mode, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised when either a WFI instruction is attempted while the TW field of the mstatus CSR is zero or a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE) is attempted.  
RULE 4: In VS‑mode, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised when a WFI instruction is attempted while the VTW field of the hstatus CSR is 1 and the TW field of the mstatus CSR is zero, unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific bounded time.  
RULE 5: In VS‑mode or VU‑mode, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised when an attempt is made to access an implemented non‑high‑half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR that would be allowed in HS‑mode, assuming the TVM field of the mstatus CSR is zero.  
RULE 6: In VS‑mode or VU‑mode with XLEN = 32, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised when an attempt is made to access an implemented high‑half hypervisor CSR or high‑half VS CSR that would be allowed for its low‑half partner in HS‑mode, assuming the TVM field of the mstatus CSR is zero.  
RULE 7: In VU‑mode, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised when an attempt is made to access an implemented non‑high‑half supervisor CSR that would be allowed in HS‑mode, assuming the TVM field of the mstatus CSR is zero.  
RULE 8: In VU‑mode with XLEN = 32, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised when an attempt is made to access an implemented high‑half supervisor CSR that would be allowed for its low‑half partner in HS‑mode, assuming the TVM field of the mstatus CSR is zero.

The following description:

- When a trap is taken into M‑mode, the architectural state is updated as follows:

  * Virtualization mode **V** is set to **0**.  
  * Fields **MPV** and **MPP** in **mstatus** (or **mstatush**) are set according to Table 52.  
  * Fields **GVA**, **MPIE**, and **MIE** in **mstatus** (or **mstatush**) are written (the text does not specify the values written).  
  * CSRs **mepc**, **mcause**, **mtval**, **mtval2**, and **mtinst** are also written.

- Table 52 – Value of **mstatus** / **mstatush** fields **MPV** and **MPP** after a trap into M‑mode.  Upon trap return, **MPV** is ignored when **MPP=3**.

| Previous Mode | MPV | MPP |
|----------------|-----|-----|
| U‑mode         | 0   | 0   |
| HS‑mode        | 0   | 1   |
| M‑mode         | 0   | 3   |
| VU‑mode        | 1   | 0   |
| VS‑mode        | 1   | 1   |

- No other statements in the provided text modify **mstatus** (or **mstatush**) for traps taken into HS‑mode or VS‑mode; only the fields in **hstatus**, **sstatus**, and **vsstatus** are mentioned for those cases.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If a trap is taken into M-mode from U-mode, the MPV field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0 and the MPP field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0.  
RULE 2: If a trap is taken into M-mode from HS-mode, the MPV field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0 and the MPP field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1.  
RULE 3: If a trap is taken into M-mode from M-mode, the MPV field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0 and the MPP field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 3.  
RULE 4: If a trap is taken into M-mode from VU-mode, the MPV field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1 and the MPP field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0.  
RULE 5: If a trap is taken into M-mode from VS-mode, the MPV field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1 and the MPP field of mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1.  
RULE 6: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the GVA, MPIE, and MIE fields of mstatus (or mstatush) are written, with the specification not defining the values written.  
RULE 7: Upon trap return, the MPV field of mstatus is ignored when the MPP field equals 3.  
RULE 8: The specification does not modify any fields of mstatus (or mstatush) for traps taken into HS-mode or VS-mode.

The following description:

- **MRET privilege‑mode determination** – The *MRET* instruction "first determines what the new privilege mode will be according to the values of **MPP** and **MPV** in **mstatus** or **mstatus h**, as encoded in **Table 52**."  

- **MRET update of mstatus / mstatus h** – After determining the new privilege mode, *MRET* "in **mstatus** / **mstatus h** sets **MPV=0**, **MPP=0**, **MIE=MPIE**, and **MPIE=1**."  

- **Fields of mstatus that affect MRET behavior** – The behavior of *MRET* is directly caused by the **MPP** and **MPV** fields (used for privilege‑mode selection) and the **MIE** and **MPIE** fields (which are written by *MRET*).  

*Table 52 is referenced for the encoding of the new privilege mode based on MPP and MPV, but its contents are not provided in the source text.*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: MRET first determines the new privilege mode from the values of the MPP and MPV fields in mstatus or mstatush according to the encoding defined in Table 52.  
RULE 2: After determining the new privilege mode, MRET updates mstatus (or mstatush) by setting MPV to 0, MPP to 0, copying MPIE into MIE, and setting MPIE to 1.  
RULE 3: The MPP and MPV fields in mstatus (or mstatush) are used by MRET to select the target privilege mode.  
RULE 4: The MIE and MPIE fields in mstatus (or mstatush) are written by MRET as part of its operation.

The following description:

- The **MPELP bit** is provided **in the `mstatus` CSR** to store the previous **ELP** state on trap delivery to **M‑mode**.  
- The **SPELP bit** is provided **in the `mstatus` CSR** to store the previous **ELP** state on trap delivery to **S/HS‑mode**.  
- The **SPELP bit** in `mstatus` **can be accessed through the `sstatus` CSR**.  
- **When a trap is taken into privilege mode x**, the **x PELP** (i.e., MPELP for M‑mode, SPELP for S/HS‑mode) **is set to ELP** and **ELP is set to `NO_LP_EXPECTED`**.  
- **When executing an x RET instruction** (MRET or SRET), **if the new privilege mode is y**, then **ELP is set to the value of x PELP if y LPE = 1; otherwise, ELP is set to `NO_LP_EXPECTED`**; additionally, **x PELP is set to `NO_LP_EXPECTED`**.  
- The **trap handler in privilege mode x must save the x PELP bit and the x7 register before performing an indirect call/jump if xLPE = 1**.  
- If privilege mode x can respond to interrupts and **xLPE = 1**, then the **trap handler should also save these values before enabling interrupts**.  
- The **trap handler in privilege mode x must restore the saved x PELP bit and the x7 register before executing the x RET instruction to return from a trap**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M‑mode.  
RULE 2: The SPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to S/HS‑mode.  
RULE 3: The SPELP bit in mstatus can be accessed through the sstatus CSR.  
RULE 4: When a trap is taken into privilege mode x, the x PELP field in mstatus (MPELP for M‑mode, SPELP for S/HS‑mode) is set to the current ELP value and the ELP field in mstatus is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 5: When executing an x RET instruction, if the target privilege mode y has LPE = 1 then the ELP field in mstatus is set to the value of the x PELP field; otherwise the ELP field in mstatus is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 6: When executing an x RET instruction, the x PELP field in mstatus is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 7: The trap handler in privilege mode x MUST save the x PELP bit from mstatus and the x7 register before performing an indirect call or jump if xLPE = 1.  
RULE 8: If privilege mode x can respond to interrupts and xLPE = 1, then the trap handler SHOULD also save the x PELP bit from mstatus and the x7 register before enabling interrupts.  
RULE 9: The trap handler in privilege mode x MUST restore the saved x PELP bit in mstatus and the x7 register before executing the x RET instruction to return from a trap.

The following description:

- The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual‑memory management operations.  

- When **TVM = 1**, attempts **to read or write the satp CSR** or **execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction** while executing in **S‑mode will raise an illegal‑instruction exception**.

RULE 2: When TVM=1, an attempt to read the satp CSR while executing in S‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: When TVM=1, an attempt to write the satp CSR while executing in S‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 4: When TVM=1, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction while executing in S‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception because the satp TVM bit is set.  
RULE 5: When TVM=1, executing an SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception because the satp TVM bit is set.  
RULE 6: When TVM=0, attempts to read or write the satp CSR and executions of SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instructions in S‑mode are permitted.  
RULE 7: The TVM bit in satp is read‑only zero on implementations that do not support S‑mode.

The following description:

- **FIOM read‑only condition** – "If S‑mode is not supported, or if **satp.MODE** is read‑only zero (always Bare), the implementation may make FIOM read‑only zero."  

- **PBMTE bit effect on page‑tables pointed to by satp** – "The **PBMTE** bit controls whether the Svpbmt extension is available for use in S‑mode and G‑stage address translation (i.e., for page tables pointed to by **satp** or **hgatp**). When **PBMTE=1**, Svpbmt is available for S‑mode and G‑stage address translation. When **PBMTE=0**, the implementation behaves as though Svpbmt were not implemented."  

- **PBMTE read‑only when Svpbmt not implemented** – "If Svpbmt is not implemented, **PBMTE** is read‑only zero."  

- **Hypervisor interaction with PBMTE** – "For implementations with the hypervisor extension, **henvcfg.PBMTE** is read‑only zero if **menvcfg.PBMTE** is zero."  

- **Cache‑translation synchronization after PBMTE change** – "After changing **menvcfg.PBMTE**, executing an **SFENCE.VMA** instruction with **rs1 = x0** and **rs2 = x0** suffices to synchronize address‑translation caches with respect to the altered interpretation of page‑table entries' **PBMT** fields."  

- **ADUE bit enables hardware updating of PTE A/D bits during S‑mode translation (satp‑controlled)** – "When **ADUE=1**, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during S‑mode address translation, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension were not implemented for S‑mode address translation."  

- **ADUE effect on G‑stage translation (hgatp‑controlled) when hypervisor present** – "When the hypervisor extension is implemented, if **ADUE=1**, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during G‑stage address translation, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension were not implemented for G‑stage address translation."  

- **ADUE = 0 disables hardware updating (Svade implemented)** – "When **ADUE=0**, the implementation behaves as though Svade were implemented for S‑mode and G‑stage address translation."  

- **ADUE read‑only when Svadu not implemented** – "If Svadu is not implemented, **ADUE** is read‑only zero."  

- **Hypervisor interaction with ADUE** – "For implementations with the hypervisor extension, **henvcfg.ADUE** is read‑only zero if **menvcfg.ADUE** is zero."  

- **Cache‑translation synchronization after ADUE change** – "After changing **menvcfg.ADUE**, executing an **SFENCE.VMA** instruction with **rs1 = x0** and **rs2 = x0** suffices to synchronize address‑translation caches with respect to the altered interpretation of page‑table entries' **A/D** bits."  

- **Svade implementation tied to ADUE** – "The Svade extension requires page‑fault exceptions be raised when PTE A/D bits need be set, hence Svade is implemented when **ADUE=0**."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When S‑mode is not supported or when satp.MODE is read‑only zero (always Bare), the implementation may make the FIOM CSR read‑only zero.  
RULE 2: When the PBMTE field is 1, the Svpbmt extension is available for S‑mode address translation of page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 3: When the PBMTE field is 0, the implementation behaves as though the Svpbmt extension is not implemented for S‑mode address translation of page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 4: If the Svpbmt extension is not implemented, the PBMTE field is read‑only zero, which controls the interpretation of page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 5: For implementations that include the hypervisor extension, henvcfg.PBMTE is read‑only zero if menvcfg.PBMTE is zero, affecting the use of Svpbmt for page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 6: After a change to menvcfg.PBMTE, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0 synchronizes address‑translation caches for page tables pointed to by satp with respect to the modified PBMT field interpretation.  
RULE 7: When ADUE is 1, hardware updates of PTE A and D bits are enabled during S‑mode address translation of page tables pointed to by satp, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension is not implemented for S‑mode address translation.  
RULE 8: When ADUE is 0, the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension is implemented for S‑mode address translation of page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 9: If the Svadu extension is not implemented, the ADUE field is read‑only zero, which governs hardware updating of PTE A/D bits for page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 10: For implementations with the hypervisor extension, henvcfg.ADUE is read‑only zero if menvcfg.ADUE is zero, affecting the ADUE‑controlled update of PTE A/D bits for page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 11: After a change to menvcfg.ADUE, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0 synchronizes address‑translation caches for page tables pointed to by satp with respect to the altered interpretation of PTE A/D bits.  
RULE 12: The Svade extension is considered implemented when ADUE is 0, requiring page‑fault exceptions for setting PTE A/D bits during address translation of page tables pointed to by satp.  
RULE 13: When the hypervisor extension is implemented and ADUE is 1, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during G‑stage address translation, the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension is not implemented for G‑stage address translation, and satp continues to control S‑mode translation.

The following description:

satp CSR is an SXLEN‑bit read/write register that controls supervisor‑mode address translation and protection. It holds the physical page number (PPN) of the root page table (the supervisor physical address divided by 4 KiB), an address‑space identifier (ASID) that facilitates address‑translation fences on a per‑address‑space basis, and a MODE field that selects the current address‑translation scheme. Further details on the access to this register are described in Section 3.1.6.6.

**Register format**

*For SXLEN = 32* (Figure 63)  

| Bits | Field | Attribute |
|------|-------|-----------|
| 31   | MODE  | WARL |
| 30‑22| ASID  | WARL |
| 21‑0 | PPN   | WARL |

*For SXLEN = 64* (Figure 64)  

| Bits | Field | Attribute |
|------|-------|-----------|
| 63‑60| MODE  | WARL |
| 59‑44| ASID  | WARL |
| 43‑0 | PPN   | WARL |

**Physical‑address implications**

* "Storing a PPN in satp, rather than a physical address, supports a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32."  
* "The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers. Some platform standards might place constraints on the values satp.PPN may assume, e.g., by requiring that all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory be representable."

**MODE field encodings** (Table 40)

*For SXLEN = 32*  

| Value | Name   | Description |
|-------|--------|-------------|
| 0 1   | Bare Sv32 | No translation or protection. Page‑based 32‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.3). |

*For SXLEN = 64*  

| Value | Name | Description |
|-------|------|-------------|
| 0     | Bare | No translation or protection. |
| 1‑7   | –    | Reserved for standard use. |
| 8     | Sv39 | Page‑based 39‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.4). |
| 9     | Sv48 | Page‑based 48‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.5). |
| 10    | Sv57 | Page‑based 57‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.6). |
| 11    | Sv64 | Reserved for page‑based 64‑bit virtual addressing. |
| 12‑13 | –    | Reserved for standard use. |
| 14‑15 | –    | Designated for custom use. |

* "When MODE = Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses, and there is no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme described in Section 3.7."  
* "To select MODE = Bare, software must write zero to the remaining fields of satp (bits 30‑0 when SXLEN = 32, or bits 59‑0 when SXLEN = 64)."  
* "Attempting to select MODE = Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining fields has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the value that the remaining fields assume and an UNSPECIFIED effect on address translation and protection behavior."  

**Reserved and custom encodings**

* "When SXLEN = 32, the satp encodings corresponding to MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7] = 3 are designated for custom use, whereas the encodings corresponding to MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7] ≠ 3 are reserved for future standard use."  
* "When SXLEN = 64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE = Bare are reserved for future standard use."  
* "Version 1.11 of this standard stated that the remaining fields in satp had no effect when MODE = Bare. Making these fields reserved facilitates future definition of additional translation and protection modes, particularly in RV32, for which all patterns of the existing MODE field have already been allocated."

**Unsupported MODE handling**

* "Implementations are not required to support all MODE settings, and if satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect; no fields in satp are modified."

**ASID length**

* "The number of ASID bits is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero."  
* "The number of implemented ASID bits, termed ASIDLEN, may be determined by writing one to every bit position in the ASID field, then reading back the value in satp to see which bit positions in the ASID field hold a one."  
* "The least‑significant bits of ASID are implemented first: that is, if ASIDLEN > 0, ASID[ASIDLEN‑1:0] is writable."  
* "The maximal value of ASIDLEN, termed ASIDMAX, is 9 for Sv32 or 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57."

**Atomicity of ASID + PPN update**

* "We store the ASID and the page table base address in the same CSR to allow the pair to be changed atomically on a context switch. Swapping them non‑atomically could pollute the old virtual address space with new translations, or vice‑versa. This approach also slightly reduces the cost of a context switch."

**Active‑state semantics**

* "The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S‑mode or U‑mode."  
* "Executions of the address‑translation algorithm may only begin using a given value of satp when satp is active."  

**Translation completion and SFENCE.VMA**

* "Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed."  
* "The SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to the address‑translation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads to those structures by a hart."  

**Ordering and cache‑invalidation guarantees**

* "Note that writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page‑table updates and subsequent address translations, nor does it imply any invalidation of address‑translation caches."  
* "If the new address space's page tables have been modified, or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp."  
* "Not imposing upon implementations to flush address‑translation caches upon satp writes reduces the cost of context switches, provided a sufficiently large ASID space."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: satp is an SXLEN‑bit read/write CSR that controls supervisor‑mode address translation and protection and holds a physical page number (PPN) of the root page table, an address‑space identifier (ASID), and a MODE field.  
RULE 2: For SXLEN = 32 the satp register fields are MODE bits[31], ASID bits[30‑22], and PPN bits[21‑0], and each field has WARL attribute.  
RULE 3: For SXLEN = 64 the satp register fields are MODE bits[63‑60], ASID bits[59‑44], and PPN bits[43‑0], and each field has WARL attribute.  
RULE 4: Storing a PPN in satp rather than a full physical address enables a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32 implementations.  
RULE 5: The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers, and platform specifications may constrain the values that satp.PPN may assume.  
RULE 6: For SXLEN = 32 a MODE value of 0 (Bare Sv32) selects a mode with no translation or protection and page‑based 32‑bit virtual addressing.  
RULE 7: For SXLEN = 64 a MODE value of 0 selects a Bare mode with no translation or protection.  
RULE 8: For SXLEN = 64 a MODE value of 1‑7 is reserved for standard use.  
RULE 9: For SXLEN = 64 a MODE value of 8 selects Sv39 page‑based 39‑bit virtual addressing.  
RULE 10: For SXLEN = 64 a MODE value of 9 selects Sv48 page‑based 48‑bit virtual addressing.  
RULE 11: For SXLEN = 64 a MODE value of 10 selects Sv57 page‑based 57‑bit virtual addressing.  
RULE 12: For SXLEN = 64 a MODE value of 11 selects Sv64, which is reserved for page‑based 64‑bit virtual addressing.  
RULE 13: For SXLEN = 64 MODE values 12‑13 are reserved for standard use.  
RULE 14: For SXLEN = 64 MODE values 14‑15 are designated for custom use.  
RULE 15: When satp.MODE = Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses and no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme is provided.  
RULE 16: To select satp.MODE = Bare, software must write zero to all remaining satp bits (bits 30‑0 when SXLEN = 32, or bits 59‑0 when SXLEN = 64).  
RULE 17: Attempting to select satp.MODE = Bare with a non‑zero pattern in the remaining bits has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the values of those bits and on address‑translation and protection behavior.  
RULE 18: For SXLEN = 32, satp encodings with MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7] = 3 are designated for custom use.  
RULE 19: For SXLEN = 32, satp encodings with MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7] ≠ 3 are reserved for future standard use.  
RULE 20: For SXLEN = 64, all satp encodings with MODE = Bare are reserved for future standard use.  
RULE 21: Implementations are not required to support all satp.MODE settings, and if satp is written with an unsupported MODE the entire write has no effect and no satp fields are modified.  
RULE 22: The number of ASID bits in satp is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero.  
RULE 23: The implemented number of ASID bits (ASIDLEN) may be discovered by writing ones to every bit position in the satp ASID field and reading back which bits retain a one.  
RULE 24: The least‑significant bits of the satp ASID field are implemented first, so that if ASIDLEN > 0 then the bits ASID[ASIDLEN‑1:0] are writable.  
RULE 25: The maximal ASIDLEN (ASIDMAX) is 9 for Sv32 and 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.  
RULE 26: The ASID and the page‑table base address are stored together in satp to allow them to be changed atomically on a context switch.  
RULE 27: The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S‑mode or U‑mode.  
RULE 28: Executions of the address‑translation algorithm may only begin using a given satp value when satp is active.  
RULE 29: Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.  
RULE 30: The SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to address‑translation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads to those structures by a hart.  
RULE 31: Writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page‑table updates and subsequent address translations, nor does it imply any invalidation of address‑translation caches.  
RULE 32: If the new address space's page tables have been modified, or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.  
RULE 33: Not requiring implementations to flush address‑translation caches on satp writes reduces the cost of context switches provided a sufficiently large ASID space.

The following description:

- **Implicit reads must follow satp**  
  Implementations **MUST** only perform implicit reads of the translation‑data structures pointed to by the current contents of the **satp** register (or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation‑data‑structure entry). Implementations **MUST** only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not those that are performed speculatively.

- **Immediate effect of satp .MODE changes**  
  Changing **satp.MODE** from Bare to other modes and vice versa **takes effect immediately**, without the need to execute an **SFENCE.VMA** instruction.

- **Immediate effect of satp .ASID changes**  
  Changes to **satp.ASID** **take effect immediately**.

- **Recycling an ASID**  
  When software recycles an ASID (i.e., re‑associates it with a different page table), it **should** first change **satp** to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID, then execute **SFENCE.VMA** with **rs1 = x0** and **rs2** set to the recycled ASID.  
  (Alternatively, software can execute the same **SFENCE.VMA** while a different ASID is loaded into **satp**, provided the next time **satp** is loaded with the recycled ASID it is simultaneously loaded with the new page table.)

- **When ASIDs are not provided or ASID 0 is always used**  
  If the implementation does not provide ASIDs, or software chooses to always use ASID 0, then after every **satp** write software **should** execute **SFENCE.VMA** with **rs1 = x0**. In the common case that no global translations have been modified, **rs2** should be set to a register other than x0 but containing the value zero, so that global translations are not flushed.

- **Read‑only‑zero satp.MODE implementations**  
  For implementations that make **satp.MODE** read‑only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an **SFENCE.VMA** instruction **might** raise an illegal‑instruction exception.

- **Virtual‑address‑width mismatch**  
  The specification states that the property allowing an implementation to use any translation valid since the most recent subsuming **SFENCE.VMA** **applies even if the virtual‑address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE**.

- **ASID locality**  
  If a hart employs an address‑translation cache, that cache **must** appear to be private to that hart. In particular, the meaning of an ASID is **local to a hart**; software may choose to use the same ASID to refer to different address spaces on different harts.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Implementations MUST only perform implicit reads of the translation‑data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation‑data‑structure entry.  
RULE 2: Implementations MUST only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not for those performed speculatively.  
RULE 3: Changing satp.MODE from Bare to another mode or from another mode to Bare takes effect immediately without executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction.  
RULE 4: Changes to satp.ASID take effect immediately.  
RULE 5: When software recycles an ASID, it SHOULD first change satp to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID and then execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID.  
RULE 6: Software may execute the same SFENCE.VMA while a different ASID is loaded into satp, provided that the next time satp is loaded with the recycled ASID it is simultaneously loaded with the new page table.  
RULE 7: If an implementation does not provide ASIDs or software always uses ASID 0, software SHOULD execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 after every satp write.  
RULE 8: In the common case where no global translations have been modified, software SHOULD set rs2 of the SFENCE.VMA executed after a satp write to a register other than x0 that contains zero so that global translations are not flushed.  
RULE 9: For implementations that make satp.MODE read‑only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 10: An implementation may use any translation that has been valid since the most recent subsuming SFENCE.VMA even if the virtual‑address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.  
RULE 11: The ASID field of satp is local to a hart, and software may use the same ASID value in satp to refer to different address spaces on different harts.

The following description:

- **Root‑page‑table address computation** – "Let a be **satp.ppn** × PAGESIZE, and let i = LEVELS‑1." (Step 1)  
  *Effect*: The value of the **ppn** field of the satp register is multiplied by the system page size to form the base physical address *a* for page‑table walks.

- **Satp activation requirement** – "The **satp** register **must be active**, i.e., the effective privilege mode must be S‑mode or U‑mode." (Step 1)  
  *Effect*: Translation proceeds only when satp is active; otherwise the translation algorithm must not be used.

- **Speculative execution condition** – "Implementations may also execute the address‑translation algorithm speculatively at any time, for any virtual address, as long as **satp is active** (as defined in Section 12.1.11)."  
  *Effect*: Speculative page‑table walks are permitted only when satp satisfies the activation condition (effective privilege mode S‑mode or U‑mode).

- **Range of addresses touched by the walker** – "The algorithm may only touch page tables reachable from the **root page table indicated in satp**, the range of addresses that an implementation's page‑table walker will touch is fully under supervisor control."  
  *Effect*: The root page‑table physical address supplied by satp.ppn limits all memory accesses performed by the page‑table walker.

- **ASID matching for address‑translation cache** – "Entries in the address‑translation cache may then satisfy subsequent step 2 reads if the **ASID** associated with the entry matches the **ASID loaded in step 0** or if the entry is associated with a global mapping." (Step 0 loads the ASID from satp)  
  *Effect*: The ASID field contained in satp (loaded at step 0) is used to determine whether a cached translation entry is applicable to a subsequent page‑table‑walk read.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The value of the satp.ppn field is multiplied by the system page size to produce the base physical address used as the root page‑table address for page‑table walks.  
RULE 2: The satp register MUST be active, meaning that the effective privilege mode must be S-mode or U-mode.  
RULE 3: Implementations MAY execute the address‑translation algorithm speculatively for any virtual address only when satp is active (effective privilege mode S-mode or U-mode).  
RULE 4: The page‑table walker may access only page‑table entries that are reachable from the root page‑table address defined by the satp register.  
RULE 5: The set of physical addresses that the page‑table walker may touch is entirely determined by the root page‑table address supplied by satp and therefore is under supervisor control.  
RULE 6: The ASID field contained in satp, loaded at step 0, is used to match address‑translation cache entries; a cache entry satisfies a subsequent read if its ASID matches this satp‑loaded ASID or if the entry is associated with a global mapping.
======================================================= END - 60 =======================================================

- **VS-mode -> access satp** – When V=1, if the processor is executing in VS‑mode and an instruction attempts to access the **satp** register, a *virtual‑instruction exception* is taken **if hstatus.VTVM = 1**.  (Source: "in VS-mode, attempts to … access satp , when hstatus .VTVM=1.")

The following description:

- The **scounteren** CSR is a 32‑bit register that **controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U‑mode**.  

- **When the CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in the scounteren register is clear**, attempts to read the **cycle**, **time**, **instret**, or **hpmcountern** register **while executing in U‑mode will cause an illegal‑instruction exception**.  

- **When one of these bits is set**, access to the corresponding register **is permitted**.  

- **scounteren must be implemented**.  

- **Any of the bits may be read‑only zero, indicating reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U‑mode**. Consequently, the bits are **effectively WARL fields**.  

- **The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable**.  

- **U‑mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in scounteren and mcounteren are both set**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The scounteren CSR is a 32-bit register that controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.  
RULE 2: When any CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the corresponding cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception.  
RULE 3: When any of these bits in scounteren is set, access to the corresponding counter register is permitted.  
RULE 4: Implementations MUST implement scounteren.  
RULE 5: Any bit in scounteren may be read-only zero, which causes reads to the corresponding counter to generate an exception when executing in U-mode.  
RULE 6: Bits in scounteren are effectively WARL fields.  
RULE 7: The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.  
RULE 8: U-mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in both scounteren and mcounteren are set.

The following description:

- When V = 1, a virtual‑instruction exception (exception code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal‑instruction exception **if the attempted instruction is HS‑qualified but is prevented from executing when V = 1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren**.

- In VU‑mode, attempts to access a non‑high‑half counter CSR cause a virtual‑instruction exception **when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1**.

- In VU‑mode, if XLEN = 32, attempts to access a high‑half counter CSR cause a virtual‑instruction exception **when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V = 1 and an HS‑qualified instruction is prevented from executing because the instruction is disabled by the scounteren CSR, a virtual‑instruction exception with exception code 22 is normally raised instead of an illegal‑instruction exception.  

RULE 2: In VU‑mode, if a non‑high‑half counter CSR is accessed and the corresponding bit in scounteren is 0 while the same bit in mcounteren is 1, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised.  

RULE 3: In VU‑mode with XLEN = 32, if a high‑half counter CSR is accessed and the corresponding bit in scounteren is 0 while the same bit in mcounteren is 1, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised.
======================================================= END - 76 =======================================================
